\displaystyle-2{\delta n}^{\alpha}\left(\mathcal{A}_{,\alpha}-\mathcal{B}_{(\alpha,\parallel)}-\mathcal{C}_{\alpha\parallel}^{\prime}\right)-\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}\left(\mathcal{A}_{,\alpha}-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{\alpha}+2\mathcal{B}_{[\alpha,\parallel]}-2\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\alpha\parallel}+2\mathcal{C}_{\alpha\parallel,\parallel}-\mathcal{C}_{\parallel\parallel,\alpha}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\delta\hat{\Gamma}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{,\alpha}-\mathcal{B}^{\prime\alpha}-\mathcal{B}_{\parallel}^{\;\;,\alpha}+\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{\;\;,\parallel}-2\mathcal{C}^{\prime\alpha}_{\parallel}+2\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\parallel,\parallel}-\mathcal{C}_{\parallel\parallel}^{\;\;\;\;,\alpha}+\delta\nu\left(2\mathcal{A}^{,\alpha}-2\mathcal{B}^{\prime\alpha}-\mathcal{B}_{\parallel}^{\;\;,\alpha}+\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{\;\;,\parallel}-2\mathcal{C}^{\prime\alpha}_{\parallel}\right)

(3.5)

\displaystyle-{\delta n}^{\gamma}\left(\mathcal{B}_{\gamma}^{\;\;,\alpha}-\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{\;\;,\gamma}+2\mathcal{C}^{\prime\alpha}_{\gamma}-4\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{(\parallel,\gamma)}+2\mathcal{C}_{\parallel\gamma}^{\;\;\;\;,\alpha}\right)+\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}+\mathcal{B}_{\parallel,\parallel}+\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\parallel\parallel}-2\mathcal{A}_{,\parallel}\right)

\displaystyle-2\mathcal{C}^{\alpha\gamma}\left(2\mathcal{C}_{\gamma\parallel,\parallel}-\mathcal{C}_{\parallel\parallel,\gamma}+\mathcal{A}_{,\gamma}-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{\gamma}-2\mathcal{B}_{[\parallel,\gamma]}-2\mathcal{C}_{\parallel\gamma}^{\prime}\right)~,

where round brackets around indices represent the symmetrization and square brackets the antisymmetrization with respect to those indices, e.g.,
2\mathcal{B}_{(\alpha,\beta)}=\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,\beta}+\mathcal{B}_{\beta,\alpha}\,
,
\,2\mathcal{B}_{[\alpha,\beta]}=\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,\beta}-\mathcal{B}_{\beta,\alpha}
.

To obtain the perturbed expression of the observer four-velocity we can use the time-like normalization
u_{\mu}u^{\mu}=-1
in Eq. (
2.9
). We derive up to second order
[
93
]

\displaystyle\delta u=-\mathcal{A}+\frac{3}{2}\mathcal{A}^{2}+{1\over 2}\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}-\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}\,,

(3.6)

and the covariant components
u_{\mu}
by using Eq. (
2.2
):

\displaystyle u_{\eta}=-a\left(1+\mathcal{A}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{A}^{2}+{1\over 2}\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\right)\,,\qquad u_{\alpha}=a\left(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}-\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}+\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}+2\mathcal{U}^{\beta}\mathcal{C}_{\alpha\beta}\right)=a\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}\,.\,\,\,

(3.7)

As our approach is purely geometric, the observer velocity
\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}
and the metric perturbations
\mathcal{A},\,\mathcal{B}_{\alpha},\,\mathcal{C}_{\alpha\beta}
cannot be constrained.
All our perturbative expressions presented in this section will ultimately depend on these independent spacetime fields.

3.2
Subtleties in second-order computations

Second-order perturbative calculations are more challenging than linear order calculations because of the many subtleties that arise.
Let us take the time lapse at the observer position as an example to clarify this point (see, e.g.
[
92
]
).
We begin by considering the differential equation (
2.44
) for the integral curve. This equation conveniently relates
\delta t
to
\delta u
so that we can exploit the second-order expression in Eq. (
3.6
) to compute the lapse in terms of metric perturbations and observer velocity only.
To perturb this differential equation we make the following consideration:
\delta t(\tau)
and
\delta u(x_{\tau})
are well-defined quantities, and their perturbative expansion can be generically expressed according to Eq. (
3.1
):

\displaystyle\delta t(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta t^{(1)}(\tau)+\delta t^{(2)}(\tau)+\cdots\,,

\displaystyle\delta u(x_{\tau})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta u^{(1)}(x_{\tau})+\delta u^{(2)}(x_{\tau})+\cdots\,.

(3.8)

However, the perturbative expansion of
\delta u(x_{\tau})
is somewhat ambiguous, as there exist different ways to define it. This quantity is in fact a functional of the observer world line, so it can be expanded around the background path
\bar{x}^{\mu}_{\tau}
as

\displaystyle\delta u(x_{\tau}=\bar{x}_{\tau}+\delta x_{\tau})=\delta u(\bar{x}_{\tau})+\delta x^{\mu}_{\tau}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}\delta u\right)\bigg\rvert_{\bar{x}_{\tau}}+\cdots\,,

(3.9)

such that the distortion in the path appears explicitly. We now make the following identification between perturbative orders, that we assume to be valid for any field on
\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle\delta u^{(1)}(x_{\tau})=\delta u^{(1)}(\bar{x}_{\tau})\,,\qquad\qquad\delta u^{(2)}(x_{\tau})=\delta u^{\mu(2)}(\bar{x}_{\tau})+\delta x^{\mu(1)}_{\tau}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}\delta u^{(1)}\right)\bigg\rvert_{\bar{x}_{\tau}}\,.

(3.10)

Note that at linear order the deviations from the background path
\bar{x}^{\mu}_{\tau}
do not come into play as
\delta u
is already a first-order perturbation. We can then write the formal perturbative solutions of Eq. (
2.44
) as

\displaystyle\delta t_{o}=\int_{0}^{\bar{t}_{\rm o}}dt\,\delta u(\bar{x}_{t})\,,

(3.11)

at linear order, and

\displaystyle\delta t_{o}=\int_{0}^{\bar{t}_{\rm o}}dt\,\left[\delta u(\bar{x}_{t})+\delta x^{\mu}_{t}(\partial_{\mu}\delta u)\big\rvert_{\bar{x}_{t}}\right]\,,

(3.12)

at second order. To emphasize the fact that the integrations are performed along the background path we use the parameterization
t
instead of
\tau
. The reason why we decide to expand each field around the background is that this allows us to better keep track of the perturbative orders, and in this way, we can actually obtain the solution
\delta x^{\mu}_{\tau}
at each perturbative order, which is then fed into a higher order solution. This choice will also prove convenient when we will compute gauge transformations.

Again, we take the time lapse at the observer position to illustrate another subtle point.
We have repeatedly stated that
\delta t(\tau)
is not a field but rather a (perturbed) time coordinate.
However, it is also possible to define the time lapse as a field along the observer world line. By specifying a point on the trajectory
x^{\mu}_{\tau}
we are also specifying a value of the field
u^{\mu}(x_{\tau})
and from Eq. (
2.44
) this also means specifying the value
\delta t(\tau)
. This procedure defines a map
\delta t(x_{\tau})
from the coordinates of the path to the corresponding time lapse such that
\delta t(x_{\tau}):=\delta t(\tau)
for all
\tau
in
[0,\tau_{o}]
.
5
5

5

In fact, we can think of the time lapse for another trajectory of a fictitious observer. In this way, it can be defined as a field on
\mathcal{M}
.
Using this field definition of the time lapse, we can replace
\delta u
in Eq. (
3.12
) by
\dot{\delta t}
, as the partial derivatives are now justified. We can now integrate by part, thus obtaining

\displaystyle\delta t_{o}=\delta x_{o}^{\mu}(\partial_{\mu}\delta t)\big\rvert_{{\bar{\rm o}}}+\int_{0}^{\bar{t}_{\rm o}}dt\,\left[\delta u(\bar{x}_{t})-\dot{\delta x}^{\mu}_{t}(\partial_{\mu}\delta t)\big\rvert_{\bar{x}_{t}}\right]\,,

(3.13)

where the initial conditions in Eq. (
2.44
) have been used. We further remark that the integral in the equation above is indeed the second-order expression for
\delta t(\bar{x}_{\rm o})
, i.e., the time lapse for another observer at
\bar{x}^{\mu}_{\rm o}
in an inhomogeneous universe. Since in the real universe the observer is located at
O
, and not at
\bar{O}
, we will need the expression for
\delta t_{o}
only, and not at all for
\delta t_{\bar{\rm o}}
. Therefore,
\delta t_{o}
will not be expanded around
\bar{x}^{\mu}_{o}
in any of the following equations while the perturbative expression in the right-hand side of Eq. (
3.13
) is expanded around the background path. The same arguments apply to
\delta x^{\alpha}_{o}
,
\delta\nu_{o}
,
{\delta n}^{\alpha}_{o}
,
\delta\nu_{s}
,
{\delta n}^{\alpha}_{s}
and all the other quantities we will compute. We conclude by stressing that we will only use the field-definition of path deviations
\delta x^{\mu}
to derive simpler equations for formal solutions, particularly to perform integration by part. On any other occurrence, they are treated simply as functions of the affine parameter denoting the path.

3.3
Time lapse and spatial shift at the observer position

Resuming the discussion, we now devote ourselves to computing perturbative expressions for the time lapse and spatial shift at the observer position. We will first derive the linear-order expression and then the second-order one. We adopt this structure throughout the whole section.

\bullet

Linear-order lapse and shift
.—
Combining the expression for
\delta u
in Eq. (
3.6
) and the linear-order solution for the time lapse at the observer in Eq. (
3.11
) we obtain at linear order

\displaystyle\delta t_{o}=-\int_{0}^{\bar{t}_{\rm o}}dt~\mathcal{A}(\bar{x}_{t})~,\qquad\qquad\delta x^{\alpha}_{o}=\int_{0}^{\bar{t}_{\rm o}}dt~\frac{1}{a}\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}(\bar{x}_{t})~.

(3.14)

Note that since the integrand is already first order in perturbations, the fields are evaluated at
\bar{x}^{\mu}_{t}
, such that the integration is along the time coordinate
t
without any spatial shift
\bar{x}^{\alpha}_{t}=0
.
We will use the parameterization
dt
to emphasize that the integration is performed along the background path.

If the observer experiences no acceleration, the geodesic equations (
2.6
) provide extra linear-order conditions

\mathcal{A}=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\left(av\right)~,\qquad\qquad v_{\alpha}=0~,

(3.15)

where we introduced the Helmholtz decomposition
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}:=v_{\alpha}-v_{,\alpha}
, with
\partial^{\alpha}v_{\alpha}=0
.
Using these geodesic conditions, the linear-order time lapse at the observer is further simplified as
[
43
,
92
,
63
]

\displaystyle\delta t_{o}=-v(\bar{x}_{\rm o})~.

(3.16)

Moreover, we derive the linear-order relations that are valid at any point along the background path:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}{\delta t}=-\mathcal{A}(\bar{x}_{t})~,\qquad\qquad\frac{\partial}{\partial t}{\delta x}^{\alpha}={1\over a}\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}(\bar{x}_{t})~.

(3.17)

\bullet

Second-order lapse and shift
.—
Combining the second-order expression for
\delta t_{o}
in Eq. (
3.13
) with
\delta u
in Eq. (
3.6
) and Eq. (
3.17
) we derive

\displaystyle\delta t_{o}=\delta x^{\mu}_{o}(\partial_{\mu}\delta t)\big\rvert_{\bar{\rm o}}+\int_{0}^{\bar{t}_{\rm o}}dt\left(-\mathcal{A}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{A}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}-\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}-\frac{1}{a}\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}\delta t_{,\alpha}\right)\bigg\rvert_{\bar{x}_{t}}~.

(3.18)

To compute the spatial shift at the observer we can follow the same steps to obtain at the second order

\displaystyle\delta x^{\alpha}_{o}=\delta x_{o}^{\mu}(\partial_{\mu}\delta x^{\alpha})\big\rvert_{\bar{\rm o}}+\int_{0}^{\bar{t}_{\rm o}}dt\,\frac{1}{a}\left(\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}+\mathcal{A}\,\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}-\mathcal{U}^{\beta}\delta x^{\alpha}{}_{,\beta}\right)\bigg\rvert_{\bar{x}_{t}}\,.

(3.19)

In both expressions, any occurrence of
\delta x^{\mu}
is intended as the linear-order expression in Eq. (
3.14
), even if we do not perform the explicit substitution. In this sense the partial derivatives
\partial_{\nu}\delta x^{\mu}
are well-defined.

The second-order expression for the time lapse can be further simplified by assuming that the observer follows a geodesic. In particular, the linear-order condition in Eq. (
3.16
) at any point of the background path reads
\delta t=-av
. Using this equation we can rearrange Eq. (
3.18
) as

\displaystyle{\delta t}_{o}=\int_{0}^{\bar{t}_{\rm o}}dt\left(-\mathcal{A}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{A}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}-\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}+\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}v_{,\alpha}\right)\bigg\rvert_{\bar{x}_{t}}+\left(\mathcal{A}v-\delta x^{\alpha}v_{,\alpha}\right)_{\bar{\rm o}}~.

(3.20)

Moreover, we derive the second-order geodesic equations from the definition in Eq. (
2.6
):

\displaystyle a_{\alpha}=0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{,\alpha}+\frac{1}{a}\left[a\left(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}-\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}\right)\right]^{\prime}+\mathcal{A}\left(-2\mathcal{A}_{,\alpha}+2\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{\alpha}+2\mathcal{H}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}-\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{\alpha}-\mathcal{H}\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}+2\mathcal{C}^{\prime\beta}_{\alpha}\mathcal{U}_{\beta}+2\mathcal{C}^{\beta}_{\alpha}\left(-\mathcal{A}_{,\beta}+\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{\beta}+\mathcal{H}\mathcal{B}_{\beta}\right)+\mathcal{U}^{\beta}\left(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha,\beta}-2\mathcal{B}_{[\alpha,\beta]}\right)\,,

\displaystyle a^{\eta}=0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}-\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}\right)a_{\alpha}\,.

(3.21)

Noting that the linear-order geodesic condition in Eq. (
3.15
) can be recast as
\mathcal{A}_{,\alpha}=-(1/a)\left(a\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}\right)^{\prime}
, we can rewrite the previous geodesic condition by using
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}
in Eq. (
3.7
):

a_{\alpha}=0=\mathcal{A}_{,\alpha}+\frac{1}{a}\left(a\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}\right)^{\prime}\left(1+\mathcal{A}\right)+\mathcal{U}^{\beta}\left(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha,\beta}+\mathcal{B}_{\beta,\alpha}\right)\,.

(3.22)

Furthermore, by considering
\mathcal{U}^{\beta}\mathcal{B}_{\beta,\alpha}=\mathcal{U}^{\beta}\mathcal{U}_{\beta,\alpha}-\mathcal{U}^{\beta}\mathcal{V}_{\beta,\alpha}
we manipulate the equation

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}_{,\alpha}+\frac{1}{a}\left(a\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}\right)^{\prime}+2\mathcal{U}^{\beta}\mathcal{V}_{[\alpha,\beta]}-\mathcal{A}\mathcal{A}_{,\alpha}+\mathcal{U}^{\beta}\mathcal{U}_{\beta,\alpha}=\left(a\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}\right)^{\cdot}+\left[\mathcal{A}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{A}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{U}^{\beta}\mathcal{U}_{\beta}\right]_{,\alpha},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\quad

(3.23)

which can be readily solved by the Helmholtz decomposition as

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{A}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{U}^{\beta}\mathcal{U}_{\beta}-{\left(av\right)}^{\cdot}=0\,,\qquad\qquad v_{\alpha}=0\,.

(3.24)

Finally, we manipulate the integrand of Eq. (
3.18
)

-\mathcal{A}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{A}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}-\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}-\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}v_{,\alpha}=-\mathcal{A}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{A}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}~,

(3.25)

such that by combining this equation with the previous one, we derive the second-order time lapse at the observer with geodesic motion

\delta t_{o}=-\left(v-\mathcal{A}v+\delta x^{\alpha}v_{,\alpha}\right)_{\bar{\rm o}}~.

(3.26)

This result is consistent with the one derived in
[
92
]
.
Due to our use of conformal time, it proves more convenient the observer time lapse
\delta\eta_{o}
in terms of the conformal time. Given the definition of the cosmic time
dt=ad\eta
, we integrate it to obtain the matching condition for
\eta_{o}=\bar{\eta}_{o}+\delta\eta_{o}
:

\displaystyle t_{o}=\bar{t}_{\rm o}+\delta t_{o}=\int_{0}^{\eta_{o}}d\eta\,a(\eta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{t}_{\rm o}+\delta\eta_{o}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}(\bar{\eta}_{\rm o})\delta\eta^{2}_{o}+\cdots\,,

(3.27)

and this yields the second-order expression for the time lapse in conformal coordinates

\displaystyle\delta\eta_{o}=\delta t_{o}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}_{\bar{\rm o}}\delta t_{o}^{2}\,.

(3.28)

3.4
Distortion in the conformal wave vector

Having set up the observer position at second order, we proceed to compute the perturbations in the conformal wave vector
\hat{k}^{\mu}
along the light path using the geodesic equations (
2.42
).

\bullet

Linear-order solution
.—
By substituting
\delta\hat{\Gamma}^{\eta}
and
\delta\hat{\Gamma}^{\alpha}
in Eq. (
3.1
) into the formal geodesic solutions in Eq. (
2.42
), we obtain at linear order

\displaystyle\delta\nu\big\rvert^{s}_{o}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{B}_{\parallel}-2\mathcal{A}\right)\bigg\rvert^{z}_{\bar{\rm o}}-\int_{0}^{\bar{r}_{z}}d\bar{r}\left(\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{B}_{\parallel}-\mathcal{C}_{\parallel\parallel}\right)^{\prime}~,

\displaystyle{\delta n}_{\alpha}\big\rvert^{s}_{o}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}+2\mathcal{C}_{\alpha\parallel}\right)\bigg\rvert^{z}_{\bar{\rm o}}-\int_{0}^{\bar{r}_{z}}d\bar{r}\left(\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{B}_{\parallel}-\mathcal{C}_{\parallel\parallel}\right)_{,\alpha}\,.

(3.29)

Recalling the arguments presented in Subsection (
3.2
), the integration is performed along the background path, parameterized by the comoving distance
\bar{r}
instead of the affine parameter
\lambda
. In fact, from Eq. (
2.58
) the following relation holds at the background

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\lambda}=-\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}={\partial\over\partial\eta}-n^{\alpha}{\partial\over\partial x^{\alpha}}\,.

(3.30)

We can obtain the linear-order expressions for
\hat{k}_{o}^{\mu}
using the tetrad basis at the observer position.
From the definition in Eq. (
2.12
) and the orthonormality condition in Eq. (
2.14
) we derive the space-like tetrad components at linear order

\displaystyle\delta e_{i}^{\eta}=\delta^{\alpha}_{i}(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}-\mathcal{B}_{\alpha})\,,\qquad\qquad S_{ij}=\delta^{\alpha}_{i}\delta^{\beta}_{j}\mathcal{C}_{\alpha\beta}\,,

(3.31)

where
S_{ij}
is the symmetric part of the spatial components
\delta e_{i}^{\alpha}
in Eq. (
2.15
). To derive the antisymmetric part
A^{j}{}_{i}
, we cannot rely on the orthonormality condition, but we can find constraints based on gauge transformation properties
[
63
]
. The derivation is shown in Appendix
A
, here we state the result:

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha j}A^{j}{}_{i}=C_{[\alpha,\beta]}\delta^{\beta}_{i}+\varepsilon_{\alpha ij}\Omega^{j}\,,

(3.32)

where
C_{\alpha}
is the vector part of
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha\beta}
in its scalar-vector-tensor decomposition,
\varepsilon_{ijk}
is the Levi-Civita symbol with
\varepsilon_{123}=1
, and
\Omega^{i}
is a gauge-invariant vector describing the misalignment of the space-like tetrad basis against a FLRW coordinate
[
63
]
.
Recall that in Eq. (
2.12
) we fully specified the tetrad basis only at background level, where we required the components of the space-like tetrads to be fully aligned with FLRW spatial directions. In the real universe
\mathcal{M}
, we fixed the three degrees of freedom associated with boosts, but we still have three degrees of freedom associated with spatial rotations that are specified by
\Omega^{i}
, but not determined by metric perturbations. Combining these results with the definition of
\hat{k}^{\mu}_{o}
in Eq. (
2.32
) we derive the linear-order distortions at the observer

\displaystyle\delta\nu_{o}=-\left(\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{U}_{\parallel}-\mathcal{B}_{\parallel}\right)_{\bar{\rm o}}\,,\qquad\qquad\left({\delta n}_{\alpha}\right)_{o}=-\left(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}+\mathcal{C}_{\alpha\parallel}+C_{[\alpha,\parallel]}+\epsilon_{\alpha ij}n^{i}\Omega^{j}\right)_{\bar{\rm o}}\,.

(3.33)

\bullet

Second-order solution
.—
To obtain the second-order solution we need to expand the formal expression in Eq. (
2.42
) around the background. This implies expanding the fields as functionals of the light path, but also expanding the upper limit of integration
\lambda_{s}
around affine parameter
\lambda_{z}
via
\lambda_{s}=\lambda_{z}+\Delta\lambda_{s}
:

\displaystyle\delta\nu\big\rvert^{s}_{o}=\int_{0}^{\bar{r}_{z}}d\bar{r}\left[\delta\hat{\Gamma}^{\eta}(\bar{x}_{\bar{r}})+\delta x^{\mu}_{\bar{r}}(\partial_{\mu}\delta\hat{\Gamma}^{\eta})\big\rvert_{\bar{x}_{\bar{r}}}\right]-\Delta\lambda_{s}\delta\hat{\Gamma}^{\eta}(\bar{x}_{z})\,.

(3.34)

We can simplify this equation using the geodesic equation (
2.42
)
\frac{d}{d\lambda}\delta\nu=-\delta\hat{\Gamma}^{\eta}
, and then integrate by part.
Using the perturbations for
\delta\hat{k}^{\mu}
in Eq. (
2.66
) and for
\Delta x_{s}^{\mu}
in Eq. (
2.60
) we arrive at

\displaystyle\delta\nu\big\rvert^{s}_{o}=\left[\Delta x^{\mu}_{\bar{r}}(\partial_{\mu}\delta\nu)\big\rvert_{\bar{x}_{\bar{r}}}\right]^{z}_{\bar{\rm o}}+\int_{0}^{\bar{r}_{z}}d\bar{r}\left(\delta\hat{\Gamma}^{\eta}+\delta\nu\delta\nu^{\prime}-{\delta n}^{\alpha}\delta\nu_{,\alpha}\right)\bigg\rvert_{\bar{x}_{\bar{r}}}\,,

(3.35)

where the boundary terms on the right-hand side appear as the difference between the terms inside the square brackets evaluated at
\bar{r}=\bar{r}_{z}
and
\bar{r}=\bar{r}_{\rm o}
.
We can repeat the same steps for
{\delta n}^{\alpha}
and derive

\displaystyle{\delta n}^{\alpha}\big\rvert^{s}_{o}=\left[\Delta x^{\mu}_{\bar{r}}(\partial_{\mu}{\delta n}^{\alpha})\big\rvert_{\bar{x}_{\bar{r}}}\right]^{z}_{\bar{\rm o}}-\int_{0}^{\bar{r}_{z}}d\bar{r}\left(\delta\hat{\Gamma}^{\alpha}-\delta\nu{\delta n}^{\prime\alpha}+{\delta n}^{\beta}{\delta n}^{\alpha}{}_{,\beta}\right)\bigg\rvert_{\bar{x}_{\bar{r}}}\,.

(3.36)

To obtain these expressions in terms of perturbations of the metric and velocity, we should substitute the linear-order solutions for
\delta\nu(\bar{x})
and
{\delta n}^{\alpha}(\bar{x})
in Eq. (
3.4
), then the second-order expressions for
\delta\hat{\Gamma}^{\eta}(\bar{x})
and
\delta\hat{\Gamma}^{\alpha}(\bar{x})
in Eq. (
3.1
), as well as the expression for
\Delta x^{\mu}_{s}
(which we will derive later) and
\delta x^{\mu}_{o}
from the previous subsection. The complete expression is presented in Appendix
B
.

Again, we can compute the components of
\hat{k}^{\mu}_{o}
using the tetrad at the observer in Eqs. (
2.12
) and (
2.15
). Employing the orthonormality condition (
2.14
) we derive at second order

\displaystyle\delta e^{\eta}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{i}-\mathcal{B}_{i}+\mathcal{A}(2\mathcal{B}_{i}-\mathcal{U}_{i})+\mathcal{C}_{i\alpha}(\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}+\mathcal{B}^{\alpha})+\varepsilon_{i\alpha j}(\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}-\mathcal{B}^{\alpha})\Omega^{j}+(\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}-\mathcal{B}^{\alpha})C_{[i,\alpha]}\,,

\displaystyle S_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{ij}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{B}_{i}\mathcal{B}_{j}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{U}_{i}\mathcal{U}_{j}-\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{i}\Omega_{j}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{ij}\Omega^{k}\Omega_{k}-\frac{3}{2}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{i}\mathcal{C}_{\alpha j}-\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{(i}\varepsilon_{j)k\alpha}\Omega^{k}-\frac{1}{2}C_{\alpha,(i}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{j)}

(3.37)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}C^{\alpha}{}_{,(i}\varepsilon_{j)k\alpha}\Omega^{k}-\frac{1}{2}C_{(i,\alpha}\varepsilon_{j)k}{}^{\alpha}\Omega^{k}+\frac{1}{8}C^{\alpha}{}_{,i}C_{\alpha,j}+\frac{1}{8}C_{i,}{}^{\alpha}C_{j,\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{(i}C_{j),\alpha}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}C^{\alpha}{}_{,(i}C_{j),\alpha}\,,

where spacetime indices have been contracted with Kronecker delta involving internal indices. From these relations, we derive the second-order expression for
\delta\nu_{o}
defined in Eq. (
2.32
)

\displaystyle\delta\nu_{o}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg(-\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{U}_{\parallel}+\mathcal{B}_{\parallel}+\frac{3}{2}\mathcal{A}^{2}-\mathcal{C}_{\alpha\parallel}\left(\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}+\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}\right)+\mathcal{A}\left(\mathcal{U}_{\parallel}-2\mathcal{B}_{\parallel}\right)-\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}

(3.38)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}+\left(\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}-\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}\right)C_{[\alpha,\parallel]}+\varepsilon_{\alpha ij}\left(\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}-\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}\right)n^{i}\Omega^{j}\bigg)_{\bar{\rm o}}+\delta x^{\mu}_{o}\left[\partial_{\mu}\left(-\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{U}_{\parallel}+\mathcal{B}_{\parallel}\right)\right]\big\rvert_{\bar{\rm o}}\,,\,\,\,\,\,\qquad

where the last term is the expansion needed to have the right-hand side evaluated at the background observer position
\bar{x}^{\mu}_{o}
. The second-order expression for
{\delta n}^{\alpha}_{o}
can also be computed from the definition in Eq. (
2.32
), taking into account the above expression for
S_{ij}

\displaystyle-{\delta n}^{\alpha}_{o}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}_{o}+\bigg(A^{\alpha}{}_{i}+\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{i}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{B}_{i}\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{U}_{i}\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{i}\Omega^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}\delta^{\alpha}_{i}\Omega^{k}\Omega_{k}-\frac{3}{2}\mathcal{C}^{\beta}_{i}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\beta}-\mathcal{C}^{\beta}_{(i}\varepsilon^{\alpha)}{}_{k\beta}\Omega^{k}

(3.39)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}C_{\beta,(i}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha)\beta}+\frac{1}{2}C^{\beta}{}_{,(i}\varepsilon^{\alpha)}{}_{k\beta}\Omega^{k}-\frac{1}{2}C_{(i,\beta}\varepsilon^{\alpha)}{}_{k}{}^{\beta}\Omega^{k}+\frac{1}{8}C^{\beta}{}_{,i}C_{\beta}{}^{,\alpha}+\frac{1}{8}C_{i,}{}^{\beta}C^{\alpha}{}_{,\beta}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{C}^{\beta}_{(i}C^{\alpha)}{}_{,\beta}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}C^{\beta}{}_{,(i}C^{\alpha)}{}_{,\beta}\bigg)_{\bar{\rm o}}n^{i}+\delta x^{\mu}_{o}\left[\partial_{\mu}\left(\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}+\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{i}n^{i}+C^{[\alpha}{}_{,i]}n^{i}+\varepsilon^{\alpha}{}_{ij}n^{i}\Omega^{j}\right)\right]\bigg\rvert_{\bar{\rm o}}\,.

Note that the second-order expression for the antisymmetric part
A^{j}{}_{i}
is not presented here, as it does not contribute to any of the second-order quantities we explicitly present in Appendix
B
.

3.5
Fluctuations in the observed redshift

The observed redshift
z
in Eq. (
2.52
) is affected by the cosmic expansion set by
a_{s}
at the source position and the fluctuations
\delta z
that include the Doppler effect and the Sachs-Wolfe effect. Here we compute the second-order expression for
\delta z
by using Eq. (
2.53
).
Following the steps in Section
3.2
, we expand the fields on the right-hand side in Eq. (
2.53
) around the background reference positions
\bar{x}_{z}
and
\bar{x}_{\rm o}
.
The scale factor at the observer position is

\displaystyle a(\eta_{o}=\bar{\eta}_{\rm o}+\delta\eta_{o})=1+\mathcal{H}_{\bar{\rm o}}\delta\eta_{o}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\bar{\rm o}}+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{\bar{\rm o}}\right)\delta\eta_{o}^{2}\,,

(3.40)

and we expand the perturbation
\widehat{\Delta\nu}
around the observer position
\bar{x}_{z}
as

\displaystyle\widehat{\Delta\nu}_{s}=\widehat{\Delta\nu}(x_{s}=\bar{x}_{z}+\Delta x_{s};x_{o})=\widehat{\Delta\nu}(\bar{x}_{z};x_{o})+\Delta x^{\mu}_{s}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}\widehat{\Delta\nu}\right)\bigg\rvert_{\bar{x}_{z}}\,.

(3.41)

We choose not to expand the reference position around
\bar{x}^{\mu}_{o}
, keeping it fixed at the real observer position
O
.
Combining these results, we obtain the second-order expression for the fluctuation in the observed redshift

\displaystyle\delta z(x_{s};x_{o})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\bar{\rm o}}\delta\eta_{o}+\widehat{\Delta\nu}(\bar{x}_{z};x_{o})+\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\bar{\rm o}}+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{\bar{\rm o}}\right)\delta\eta_{o}^{2}+\mathcal{H}_{\bar{\rm o}}\delta\eta_{o}\widehat{\Delta\nu}(\bar{x}_{z};x_{o})+\Delta x^{\mu}_{s}(\partial_{\mu}\widehat{\Delta\nu})\big\rvert_{\bar{x}_{z}}\,.\,\,\,\,\qquad

(3.42)

In order to express
\delta z
in terms of the metric perturbations and the observer velocity, we first need to derive a second-order expression for
\widehat{\Delta\nu}
. This is readily obtained from the exact relation in Eq. (
2.54
):

\displaystyle\widehat{\Delta\nu}(\bar{x}_{z};x_{o})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta\nu+\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{U}_{\parallel}-\mathcal{B}_{\parallel}+\delta\nu\mathcal{A}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{A}^{2}+\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}_{\parallel}+{\delta n}_{\alpha}\left(\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}-\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}+2\mathcal{C}_{\alpha\parallel}\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}\,,\,\,\,\,\qquad

(3.43)

where both sides are evaluated at
\bar{x}^{\mu}_{z}
, with reference
x^{\mu}_{o}
. The full expression for
\delta z
is given in Appendix
B
(see also
[
92
]
).

3.6
Distortion in the source position compared to the observed position

In Eq. (
2.59
) we introduced two ways to define deviations in the source position,
\delta x_{s}^{\mu}
and
\Delta x^{\mu}_{s}
. The first is a deviation compared to the unphysical background position
\bar{x}^{\mu}_{s}
, while the latter is compared to the background observed position
\bar{x}^{\mu}_{z}
. Our interest is indeed in computing the second-order perturbative expression for
\Delta x^{\mu}_{s}
, since it contains the entire chart dependence of the source position
x^{\mu}_{s}
.

\bullet

Linear-order source position
.—
To compute the temporal distortion
\Delta\eta_{s}
, we expand up to first order the exact relationship with the redshift
\delta z
in Eq. (
2.62
). We obtain

\displaystyle\Delta\eta_{s}=\frac{\delta z}{\mathcal{H}_{z}}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{H}_{z}}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\bar{\rm o}}\delta\eta_{o}+\delta\nu+\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{U}_{\parallel}-\mathcal{B}_{\parallel}\right)\,,

(3.44)

where we substituted the linear-order expression for
\delta z
computed in the previous subsection.
For the spatial components of the distortion
\Delta x^{\alpha}_{s}
, from the exact relation in Eq. (
2.70
) we derive instead

\displaystyle\Delta x_{s}^{\alpha}=\delta x_{o}^{\alpha}-n^{\alpha}(\Delta\eta_{s}-\delta\eta_{o})+\int_{0}^{\bar{r}_{z}}d\bar{r}\left({\delta n}^{\alpha}-n^{\alpha}\delta\nu\right)\,,

(3.45)

as well as the following linear-order relation valid at any point along the background light path

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}\left(\Delta x^{\alpha}+n^{\alpha}\Delta\eta\right)=\left({\delta n}^{\alpha}-n^{\alpha}\delta\nu\right)\,.

(3.46)

We stress that the left-hand side of this equation is evaluated at a given comoving distance
\bar{r}
, while the right-hand side at the background position
\bar{x}^{\mu}_{\bar{r}}
. In analogy with the observer lapse discussed in Subsection
3.2
, we can define the coordinate distortion
\Delta x^{\mu}(\bar{x}_{\bar{r}})
as a field along the background path, instead of a function of affine parameter.

Given the physical parameterization of the source position in Eq. (
2.65
), we can use the previous results to compute the radial and transverse distortions:

\displaystyle\delta r

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n_{\alpha}\Delta x^{\alpha}_{s}=n_{\alpha}\delta x_{o}^{\alpha}-\Delta\lambda_{s}+\int_{0}^{\bar{r}_{z}}d\bar{r}\,{\delta n}_{\parallel}

(3.47)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta x_{o\parallel}+\delta\eta_{o}-{\delta z\over\mathcal{H}_{z}}+\int_{0}^{\bar{r}_{z}}d\bar{r}~\left(\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{B}_{\parallel}-\mathcal{C}_{\parallel\parallel}\right)~,

\displaystyle\bar{r}_{z}\delta\theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\theta_{\alpha}\Delta x^{\alpha}_{s}=\theta_{\alpha}\delta x_{o}^{\alpha}+\int_{0}^{\bar{r}_{z}}d\bar{r}\,\theta_{\alpha}{\delta n}^{\alpha}

(3.48)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\bar{r}_{z}}d\bar{r}~\left[\theta_{\alpha}\left(\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}+2~\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\parallel}\right)+\left({\bar{r}_{z}-\bar{r}\over\bar{r}}\right){\partial\over\partial\theta}\left(\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{B}_{\parallel}-\mathcal{C}_{\parallel\parallel}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\theta_{\alpha}\delta x_{o}^{\alpha}+\bar{r}_{z}\theta_{\alpha}\left({\delta n}^{\alpha}+\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}+2~\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\parallel}\right)_{\bar{\rm o}}\,,

\displaystyle\bar{r}_{z}\sin\theta\delta\phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi_{\alpha}\Delta x^{\alpha}_{s}=\phi_{\alpha}\delta x_{o}^{\alpha}+\int_{0}^{\bar{r}_{z}}d\bar{r}\,\phi_{\alpha}{\delta n}^{\alpha}

(3.49)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\bar{r}_{z}}d\bar{r}~\left[\phi_{\alpha}\left(\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}+2~\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\parallel}\right)+\left({\bar{r}_{z}-\bar{r}\over\bar{r}\sin\theta}\right){\partial\over\partial\phi}\left(\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{B}_{\parallel}-\mathcal{C}_{\parallel\parallel}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\phi_{\alpha}\delta x_{o}^{\alpha}+\bar{r}_{z}\phi_{\alpha}\left({\delta n}^{\alpha}+\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}+2~\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\parallel}\right)_{\bar{\rm o}}\,,

where we used
\bar{x}^{\alpha}_{z}=\bar{r}_{z}n^{\alpha}(\theta,\phi)
to recast the partial derivatives.

\bullet

Second-order source position
.—
Expanding up to second order the exact relation between
\Delta\eta_{s}
and
\delta z
in Eq. (
2.62
) we obtain

\displaystyle\delta z=\mathcal{H}_{z}\Delta\eta_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{H}^{2}_{z}+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{z}\right)\Delta\eta^{2}_{s}\,,

(3.50)

again, we used the fact that
\delta z
has functional dependence only on
x^{\mu}_{s}
, not on
x^{\mu}_{o}
.
Substituting the linear-order expression for
\Delta\eta_{s}
we can solve the above equation for the second-order temporal distortion:

\displaystyle\Delta\eta_{s}=\frac{\delta z}{\mathcal{H}_{z}}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathcal{H}^{2}_{z}+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{z}}{\mathcal{H}_{z}}\left(\frac{\delta z}{\mathcal{H}_{z}}\right)^{2}\,.

(3.51)

For the spatial distortion
\Delta x^{\alpha}_{s}
we perturb the exact definition in Eq. (
2.70
) in the same way we did for
\delta\nu
and
{\delta n}^{\alpha}
, i.e., we expand around the background both the integrand and the integration limit. We obtain the following result

\displaystyle\Delta x_{s}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\bar{r}_{z}}d\bar{r}\left[{\delta n}^{\alpha}-n^{\alpha}\delta\nu+\delta x^{\mu}_{\bar{r}}\partial_{\mu}\left({\delta n}^{\alpha}-n^{\alpha}\delta\nu\right)\big\rvert_{\bar{x}_{\bar{r}}}\right]

(3.52)

\displaystyle+\delta x_{o}^{\alpha}-n^{\alpha}(\Delta\eta_{s}-\delta\eta_{o})-\Delta\lambda_{s}\left({\delta n}^{\alpha}-n^{\alpha}\delta\nu\right)\big\rvert_{\bar{x}_{z}}\,.

We can further simplify this expression using Eq. (
3.46
) to replace
{\delta n}^{\alpha}-n^{\alpha}\delta\nu
evaluated along the background path:

\displaystyle\Delta x_{s}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\bar{r}_{z}}d\bar{r}\left[{\delta n}^{\alpha}-n^{\alpha}\delta\nu+\delta x^{\mu}_{\bar{r}}\partial_{\mu}\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}(\Delta x^{\alpha}+n^{\alpha}\Delta\eta)\right]\bigg\rvert_{\bar{x}_{\bar{r}}}

(3.53)

\displaystyle+\delta x_{o}^{\alpha}-n^{\alpha}(\Delta\eta_{s}-\delta\eta_{o})-\Delta\lambda_{s}\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}(\Delta x^{\alpha}+n^{\alpha}\Delta\eta)\big\rvert_{\bar{x}_{z}}\,,

where the field definition of
\delta x^{\mu}
has been used.
We can now integrate by part and, making use of Eqs. (
2.60
) and (
2.66
), we can manipulate the above equation to finally obtain

\displaystyle\Delta x_{s}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\bar{r}_{z}}d\bar{r}\left[{\delta n}^{\alpha}-n^{\alpha}\delta\nu+\delta\nu\left(\Delta x^{\prime\alpha}+n^{\alpha}\Delta\eta^{\prime}\right)-{\delta n}^{\beta}\left(\Delta x^{\alpha}{}_{,\beta}+n^{\alpha}\Delta\eta_{,\beta}\right)\right]\bigg\rvert_{\bar{x}_{\bar{r}}}

(3.54)

\displaystyle+\delta x_{o}^{\alpha}-n^{\alpha}(\Delta\eta_{s}-\delta\eta_{o})+\left[\Delta x_{\bar{r}}^{\mu}\,\partial_{\mu}\left(\Delta x^{\alpha}+n^{\alpha}\Delta\eta\right)\big\rvert_{\bar{x}_{\bar{r}}}\right]^{z}_{\bar{\rm o}}\,,

where the partial derivatives of
\Delta x^{\mu}
act on the linear-order expression, and the square brackets represent the difference between the terms evaluated at
\bar{r}_{z}
and
\bar{r}_{\rm o}
.

We can proceed to compute the radial and transverse distortions as before. By a direct computation we find

\displaystyle\delta r

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n_{\alpha}\Delta x^{\alpha}_{s}+\frac{\bar{r}_{z}}{2}\left(\delta\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta~\delta\phi^{2}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\bar{r}_{z}\delta\theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\theta_{\alpha}\Delta x^{\alpha}_{s}-\delta r\delta\theta+\frac{\bar{r}_{z}}{2}\cos\theta\sin\theta~\delta\phi^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\bar{r}_{z}\sin\theta\delta\phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi_{\alpha}\Delta x^{\alpha}_{s}-\sin\theta~\delta r\delta\phi-\bar{r}_{z}\cos\theta~\delta\theta\delta\phi\,,

(3.55)

where again we do not make any substitution, but we derived explicit expressions in terms of metric and observer velocity perturbations for each term appearing.

3.7
Distortion in the physical volume and area

We conclude the section by presenting the second-order expressions for the fluctuations in the volume and area occupied by the source in its rest frame compared to the observed volume and area. The relevant nonlinear equations are Eq. (
2.80
) for
\delta V
and Eq. (
2.83
) for
\delta A
. The distortion in the observed proper area of the source
\delta A
is related to the perturbations
\delta\mathcal{D}
in the luminosity distance or angular diameter distance via Eq. (
2.85
).

For convenience we introduce the distortion matrix
\mathbb{D}
, usually employed in the standard formalism of gravitational lensing to compare the source angular position
(\theta+\delta\theta,\phi+\delta\phi)
with the observed angular position
(\theta,\phi)

[
93
]
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}:=\frac{\partial(\theta+\delta\theta,\phi+\delta\phi)}{\partial(\theta,\phi)}\,.

(3.56)

Given this definition, we can compute the determinant of the distortion matrix

\displaystyle{\rm det}\,\mathbb{D}=\frac{\sin(\theta+\delta\theta)}{\sin\theta}\left(1+{\partial\over\partial\theta}\delta\theta+{\partial\over\partial\phi}\delta\phi+{\partial\over\partial\theta}\delta\theta{\partial\over\partial\phi}\delta\phi-{\partial\over\partial\phi}\delta\theta{\partial\over\partial\theta}\delta\phi\right)=:1+\delta\mathbb{D}\,,

(3.57)

where the nonlinear perturbation
\delta\mathbb{D}
has been introduced. Here we introduced the distortion matrix
\mathbb{D}
merely to simplify the second-order expression for
\delta V
and
\delta A
, which would be too lengthy otherwise. At linear order, the perturbation
\delta\mathbb{D}
reads

\displaystyle\delta\mathbb{D}=\left(\cot\theta+{\partial\over\partial\theta}\right)\delta\theta+{\partial\over\partial\phi}\delta\phi=:-2\kappa\,,

(3.58)

with
\kappa
being the standard gravitational
lensing convergence describing the linear-order lensing magnification. Already apparent at the linear order,
\delta\mathbb{D}
is gauge-dependent. At second order we obtain

\displaystyle\delta\mathbb{D}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\cot\theta+{\partial\over\partial\theta}\right)\delta\theta+{\partial\over\partial\phi}\delta\phi+{\partial\over\partial\theta}\delta\theta{\partial\over\partial\phi}\delta\phi-{\partial\over\partial\phi}\delta\theta{\partial\over\partial\theta}\delta\phi+\cot\theta\,\delta\theta\left({\partial\over\partial\theta}\delta\theta+{\partial\over\partial\phi}\delta\phi\right)-\frac{1}{2}\delta\theta^{2}\,.

To derive the second-order expressions for
\delta V
and
\delta A
we first need to expand up to second-order the physical parameterization of the source position in Eq. (
2.65
), which together with

\displaystyle{\partial\over\partial z}\bar{\eta}_{z}=-\frac{1}{H_{z}}\,,\qquad\qquad{\partial\over\partial z}\bar{r}_{z}=\frac{1}{H_{z}}\,,

(3.60)

obtained from the definition of the comoving distance
\bar{r}_{z}
in Eq. (
2.58
), allows us to recast the terms in Eq. (
2.78
) for
{\rm d}V
.
From direct computation we obtain

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\eta\alpha\beta\gamma}~{\partial x^{\alpha}\over\partial z}{\partial x^{\beta}\over\partial\theta}{\partial x^{\gamma}\over\partial\phi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bar{r}^{2}_{z}\sin\theta\over H_{z}}\Bigg\{1+2~{\delta r\over\bar{r}_{z}}+\delta\mathbb{D}+H_{z}{\partial\over\partial z}~\delta r+{\delta r^{2}\over\bar{r}^{2}_{z}}+2~{\delta r\over\bar{r}_{z}}\left(H_{z}{\partial\over\partial z}~\delta r-2~\kappa\right)

(3.61)

\displaystyle-2\kappa~H_{z}{\partial\over\partial z}\delta r-H_{z}{\partial\over\partial z}~\delta\theta~{\partial\over\partial\theta}~\delta r-H_{z}{\partial\over\partial z}~\delta\phi~{\partial\over\partial\phi}~\delta r\Bigg\}~,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\alpha\mu\nu\rho}~\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}~{\partial x^{\mu}\over\partial z}{\partial x^{\nu}\over\partial\theta}{\partial x^{\rho}\over\partial\phi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bar{r}^{2}_{z}\sin\theta\over H_{z}}\Bigg\{\mathcal{U}_{\parallel}\left(1+2~{\delta r\over\bar{r}_{z}}-2~\kappa-H_{z}{\partial\over\partial z}~\Delta\eta\right)+\mathcal{U}_{\theta}~\delta\theta+\mathcal{U}_{\phi}\sin\theta~\delta\phi

(3.62)

\displaystyle-{1\over\bar{r}_{z}}\left(\mathcal{U}_{\theta}{\partial\over\partial\theta}+{\mathcal{U}_{\phi}\over\sin\theta}{\partial\over\partial\phi}\right)\left(\delta r+\Delta\eta\right)\Bigg\}~,

where we suppressed the subscript
s
. Substituting these equations in the definition of
\delta V
in Eq. (
2.80
) yields

\displaystyle\delta V

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3\delta z+\delta g+2{\delta r\over\bar{r}_{z}}+\delta\mathbb{D}+H_{z}{\partial\over\partial z}\delta r+\delta u+\mathcal{U}_{\parallel}+3\,\delta z\delta g+3~\delta z^{2}-\mathcal{A}\left(2\,{\delta r\over\bar{r}_{z}}-2\kappa+H_{z}{\partial\over\partial z}\delta r\right)

(3.63)

\displaystyle+{\delta r^{2}\over\bar{r}^{2}_{z}}+2{\delta r\over\bar{r}_{z}}\left(H_{z}{\partial\over\partial z}\delta r-2\kappa\right)-2\kappa H_{z}{\partial\over\partial z}\delta r-H_{z}{\partial\over\partial z}\delta\theta{\partial\over\partial\theta}\delta r-H_{z}{\partial\over\partial z}\delta\phi{\partial\over\partial\phi}\delta r+\mathcal{U}_{\theta}\delta\theta

\displaystyle+\mathcal{U}_{\parallel}\left(2{\delta r\over\bar{r}_{z}}-2\kappa-H_{z}{\partial\over\partial z}\Delta\eta\right)-{1\over\bar{r}_{z}}\left(\mathcal{U}_{\theta}{\partial\over\partial\theta}+{\mathcal{U}_{\phi}\over\sin\theta}{\partial\over\partial\phi}\right)\left(\delta r+\Delta\eta\right)+\mathcal{U}_{\phi}\sin\theta\delta\phi

\displaystyle+(\delta g+3~\delta z)\left(2{\delta r\over\bar{r}_{z}}-2\kappa+H_{z}{\partial\over\partial z}\delta r-\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{U}_{\parallel}\right)+\Delta x^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\left(3\,\delta z+\delta g-\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{U}_{\parallel}\right)~,

where the left-hand side is evaluated at the source position
\delta V(x_{s})
, while the right-hand side is evaluated at the background reference position
\bar{x}^{\mu}_{z}
. The last bracket expresses the expansion of the fields around these coordinates; once again, we point out that
\delta r
,
\delta\theta
,
\delta\phi
, and
\Delta\eta
are not fields, so they do not contribute to such expansion. To keep the expression as concise as possible, we did not express all the second-order terms and most of the linear-order ones.
A further complication in the expression above is that spacetime fields and observed angles mix; both derivatives concerning observables and spacetime coordinates also appear. These aspects will be treated in more detail when we consider gauge transformations in the next section.

We can proceed in the same way to compute the distortion in the observed proper area element of the source. From direct computation, we derive

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\alpha\mu\nu\rho}~\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}\left(an^{\mu}\right){\partial x^{\nu}\over\partial\theta}{\partial x^{\rho}\over\partial\phi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{r}_{z}^{2}\sin\theta\Bigg\{-\mathcal{U}_{\parallel}\left(\widehat{\Delta\nu}-\mathcal{A}-\delta\nu\right)-{1\over\bar{r}_{z}}\left(\mathcal{U}_{\theta}{\partial\over\partial\theta}+{\mathcal{U}_{\phi}\over\sin\theta}{\partial\over\partial\phi}\right)\Delta\eta\Bigg\}~,\,\,\,\,\qquad

(3.64)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\eta\alpha\beta\gamma}\left(an^{\alpha}_{s}\right){\partial x^{\beta}_{s}\over\partial\theta}{\partial x^{\gamma}_{s}\over\partial\phi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{r}_{z}^{2}\sin\theta\Bigg\{1+\mathcal{U}_{\parallel}+{\delta n}_{\parallel}-\widehat{\Delta\nu}+2~{\delta r\over\bar{r}_{z}}+\delta\mathbb{D}+2~{\delta r\over\bar{r}_{z}}~\left(\mathcal{U}_{\parallel}+{\delta n}_{\parallel}-\widehat{\Delta\nu}\right)

(3.65)

\displaystyle-2~\kappa\left(2~{\delta r\over\bar{r}_{z}}+\mathcal{U}_{\parallel}+{\delta n}_{\parallel}-\widehat{\Delta\nu}\right)+{\delta r^{2}\over\bar{r}^{2}_{z}}-\frac{1}{2}\delta\theta^{2}-{1\over 2}\left(\sin\theta~\delta\phi\right)^{2}

\displaystyle-{1\over\bar{r}_{z}}\left[\left(\mathcal{U}_{\theta}+{\delta n}_{\theta}\right){\partial\over\partial\theta}+{\left(\mathcal{U}_{\phi}+{\delta n}_{\phi}\right)\over\sin\theta}{\partial\over\partial\phi}\right]\delta r+\left(\mathcal{U}_{\phi}+{\delta n}_{\phi}\right)\sin\theta~\delta\phi

\displaystyle+\left(\mathcal{U}_{\theta}+{\delta n}_{\theta}\right)\delta\theta+\left(\delta\theta{\partial\over\partial\theta}+\delta\phi{\partial\over\partial\phi}\right){\delta r\over\bar{r}_{z}}+\widehat{\Delta\nu}\left(\widehat{\Delta\nu}-{\delta n}_{\parallel}\right)\Bigg\}~,\,\,\,\,\,\,\qquad

which can be further simplified using the linear-order null condition in Eq. (
2.35
) and the linear-order expression for
\widehat{\Delta\nu}
in Eq. (
3.43
). Substituting these results in Eq. (
2.83
) for
\delta A
we obtain

\displaystyle\delta A

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\delta z+\delta g+2{\delta r\over\bar{r}_{z}}+\delta\mathbb{D}+{\delta n}_{\parallel}+\delta u+\mathcal{U}_{\parallel}-\widehat{\Delta\nu}-\mathcal{A}\left(-2\kappa+2\frac{\delta r}{\bar{r}_{z}}-\mathcal{C}_{\parallel\parallel}\right)+\mathcal{U}_{\parallel}\left(\mathcal{B}_{\parallel}-\mathcal{U}_{\parallel}\right)

(3.66)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\bar{r}_{z}}\left(\mathcal{U}_{\theta}{\partial\over\partial\theta}+\frac{\mathcal{U}_{\phi}}{\sin\theta}{\partial\over\partial\phi}\right)\Delta\eta-2\kappa\left(2\frac{\delta r}{\bar{r}_{z}}-\mathcal{C}_{\parallel\parallel}\right)-2\frac{\delta r}{\bar{r}_{z}}\mathcal{C}_{\parallel\parallel}+\frac{\delta r^{2}}{\bar{r}^{2}_{z}}-\frac{1}{2}\delta\theta^{2}-\frac{1}{2}(\sin\theta\,\delta\phi)^{2}

\displaystyle+\widehat{\Delta\nu}\left(\mathcal{U}_{\parallel}+\mathcal{C}_{\parallel\parallel}\right)+\delta\theta\left(\mathcal{U}_{\theta}+{\delta n}_{\theta}\right)-\frac{1}{\bar{r}_{z}}\left[\left(\mathcal{U}_{\theta}+{\delta n}_{\theta}\right){\partial\over\partial\theta}+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\left(\mathcal{U}_{\phi}+{\delta n}_{\phi}\right){\partial\over\partial\phi}\right]\delta r

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\bar{r}_{z}}\delta\theta{\partial\over\partial\theta}\delta r+\sin\theta\,\delta\phi\left(\mathcal{U}_{\phi}+{\delta n}_{\phi}\right)+\frac{1}{\bar{r}_{z}}\delta\phi{\partial\over\partial\phi}\delta r+\delta z^{2}+2\,\delta z\delta g

\displaystyle+\left(2\,\delta z+\delta g\right)\left(-\mathcal{A}+2\frac{\delta r}{\bar{r}_{z}}-2\kappa-\mathcal{C}_{\parallel\parallel}\right)+\Delta x^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\left(2\,\delta z+\delta g-\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{U}_{\parallel}+{\delta n}_{\parallel}-\widehat{\Delta\nu}\right)\,,\,\,\,\,\qquad

The same remarks made for
\delta V
apply equivalently to
\delta A
. Similar expressions are derived in
[
86
,
87
,
91
,
109
,
95
,
96
,
97
]
. In Section
5
we compare our results with previous work.

4
Second-order Gauge Transformations

To test the validity of the second-order expressions derived in the previous section, we will now turn our attention to the gauge transformation properties of each of those expressions.
Resuming the discussion of Subsection
2.10
, we can compute the gauge transformation of a tensor using Eq. (
2.90
), which at second order reads

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}^{\mu\cdots\nu}_{\rho\cdots\sigma}(\widetilde{x}_{p}=x_{p})=K^{\mu\cdots\nu}_{\rho\cdots\sigma}(x_{p})-\left(\pounds_{\zeta}K^{\mu\cdots\nu}_{\rho\cdots\sigma}\right)(x_{p})+\frac{1}{2}\left(\pounds^{2}_{\zeta}K^{\mu\cdots\nu}_{\rho\cdots\sigma}\right)(x_{p})\,.

(4.1)

Recall that we evaluate both sides of the equation at value
x_{p}^{\mu}
so that the background contributions of the tensor
\bar{K}^{\mu\cdots\nu}_{\rho\cdots\sigma}(t_{p})
are identical in the two frames.
As anticipated, the gauge field
\zeta(x)
is treated as a perturbation, and it is expanded according to Eq. (
3.1
):

\displaystyle\zeta^{\mu}(x)=\zeta^{\mu(1)}(x)+\zeta^{\mu(2)}(x)+\cdots\,,

(4.2)

the same kind of expansion holds for the gauge field
\xi^{\mu}(x)
, which will be our preferred choice since it makes computations simpler. From Eqs. (
2.87
) and (
2.91
), the second-order relation between the two gauge fields is given by

\displaystyle\zeta^{\mu}=\xi^{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\xi^{\nu}{\partial\over\partial x^{\nu}}\xi^{\mu}\,,

(4.3)

which can be expressed in components using Eq. (
2.92
):

\displaystyle\zeta^{\eta}=T-\frac{1}{2}T^{\prime}T-\frac{1}{2}T_{,\alpha}\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}\,,\qquad\qquad\zeta^{\alpha}=\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}^{\prime\alpha}T-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}{}_{,\beta}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}\,.

(4.4)

With these relations we can readily recast the second-order gauge transformation in Eq. (
4.1
) in terms of our preferred gauge field
\xi^{\mu}
. Since we will deal only with scalars, vectors, and symmetric tensors with two covariant indices, the only transformation laws we need to consider are:

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K-\xi^{\mu}K_{,\mu}+\xi^{\mu}\xi^{\nu}{}_{,\mu}K_{,\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\xi^{\mu}\xi^{\nu}K_{,\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K^{\mu}-\pounds_{\xi}K^{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}\pounds^{2}_{\xi}K^{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}\xi^{\rho}\xi^{\nu}{}_{,\rho}K^{\mu}{}_{,\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\xi^{\rho}{}_{,\nu}\xi^{\mu}{}_{,\rho}+\xi^{\rho}\xi^{\mu}{}_{,\nu\rho}\right)K^{\nu}

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{\mu\nu}-\pounds_{\xi}K_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\pounds^{2}_{\xi}K_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\xi^{\sigma}\xi^{\rho}{}_{,\sigma}K_{\mu\nu,\rho}+K_{\rho(\mu}\left[\xi^{\sigma}{}_{,\nu)}\xi^{\rho}{}_{,\sigma}+\xi^{\sigma}\xi^{\rho}{}_{,\nu)\sigma}\right]\,,

(4.5)

where the Lie derivative is expressed in terms of
\xi^{\mu}
and both sides are evaluated at the same coordinate value
x^{\mu}_{p}
. Notice that while a scalar field does not transform under a change of coordinates, it indeed transforms non-trivially under a gauge transformation. In general, we are interested in the gauge transformation of the perturbation variables. Splitting a tensor
K
into the background and the perturbation
K=\bar{K}+\delta K
we derive the gauge transformation law for the perturbations:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\delta K}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta K-\bar{K}^{\prime}T-\xi^{\mu}\delta K_{,\mu}+\bar{K}^{\prime}T_{,\mu}\xi^{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}\bar{K}^{\prime\prime}T^{2}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\delta K}{}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta K^{\mu}-\bar{K}^{\prime\mu}(T-T_{,\nu}\xi^{\nu})+\bar{K}^{\nu}(\xi^{\mu}{}_{,\nu}-\xi^{\rho}\xi^{\mu}{}_{,\nu\rho})+\xi^{\mu}{}_{,\nu}\delta K^{\nu}-\xi^{\nu}\delta K^{\mu}{}_{,\nu}-\bar{K}^{\prime\nu}T\xi^{\mu}{}_{,\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\bar{K}^{\prime\prime\mu}T^{2}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\delta K}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta K_{\mu\nu}-2\bar{K}_{\rho(\mu}\xi^{\rho}{}_{,\nu)}-T\bar{K}_{\mu\nu}-\xi^{\rho}\delta K_{\mu\nu,\rho}-2\delta K_{\rho(\mu}\xi^{\rho}{}_{,\nu)}+2\bar{K}_{\rho(\mu}\left[\xi^{\rho}{}_{,\nu)\sigma}\xi^{\sigma}+\xi^{\sigma}{}_{,\nu)}\xi^{\rho}{}_{,\sigma}\right]

(4.6)

\displaystyle+\bar{K}_{\rho\sigma}\xi^{\rho}{}_{,\mu}\xi^{\sigma}{}_{,\nu}+2\bar{K}^{\prime}_{\rho(\mu}\xi^{\rho}{}_{,\nu)}T+\frac{1}{2}\bar{K}^{\prime\prime}_{\mu\nu}T^{2}+\bar{K}^{\prime}_{\mu\nu}T_{,\rho}\xi^{\rho}\,,

where background quantities are purely time-dependent due to the symmetries of the FLRW solution.

In Eq. (
2.59
) we parameterized the source position in terms of the observed position
\bar{x}^{\mu}(\lambda_{z})
parameterized by the observed redshift and angles

\displaystyle\bar{x}^{\mu}(\lambda_{z})=\left(\bar{\eta}_{z},\bar{r}_{z}n^{\alpha}\right)^{\mu}\qquad\qquad n^{\alpha}=\left(\sin\theta\cos\phi,\sin\theta\sin\phi,\cos\phi\right)^{\alpha}\,,

(4.7)

and its deviation
\Delta x^{\mu}
around the observed position.
Here we emphasize why this is more advantageous than the splitting around
\bar{x}^{\mu}(\lambda_{s})
. As we have extensively discussed, the observed redshift
z
and the observed angles
\theta,\phi
are independent of our FLRW coordinate description; hence they are scalars. From Eqs. (
2.57
) and (
2.58
) we note that these scalars fully determine the background source position
\bar{x}^{\mu}_{z}
. Therefore, the reference coordinates
\bar{x}^{\mu}_{z}
will not be affected by diffeomorphisms of
\mathcal{M}
. On the contrary, the coordinates
\bar{x}^{\mu}_{s}
of the source will be affected by the coordinate transformation, so the parameterization of the source position in terms of deviations around these coordinates is not a convenient choice to deal with gauge transformations. Given the
boundary relations in Eq. (
2.61
), the background observer position
\bar{x}^{\mu}(\lambda_{o})=(\bar{\eta}_{\rm o},0)^{\mu}
also remains unaffected by a change of coordinates. With these remarks, we can readily see that the transformations induced by the gauge field
\xi
in Eq. (
2.91
) will involve only the deviations
\Delta x^{\mu}_{s}
and
\delta x^{\mu}_{o}
, such that we can readily derive the second-order gauge transformation

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\Delta}x}^{\mu}_{s}=\Delta x^{\mu}_{s}+\xi^{\mu}(\bar{x}_{z})+\Delta x^{\nu}_{s}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\nu}}\xi^{\mu}\right)\bigg\rvert_{\bar{x}_{z}}\,,\qquad{\widetilde{\delta x}}^{\mu}_{o}=\delta x_{o}^{\mu}+\xi^{\mu}(\bar{x}_{\rm o})+\delta x^{\nu}_{o}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\nu}}\xi^{\mu}\right)\bigg\rvert_{\bar{x}_{\rm o}}\,,\qquad

(4.8)

where the fields are expanded as usual around the background reference points.

In the rest of the section, we will use gauge transformations as a consistency check of the second-order expressions derived in Section
3
. First, we compute their
expected
gauge transformations employing the nonlinear relations and exact definitions given in Section
2
. Second, we directly compute the gauge transformations of the second-order expressions in Section
3
in terms of metric perturbations and observer velocity. The consistency of these two independent procedures provides a strong validity test for our second-order expressions in Section
3
. For instance, the gauge transformation of
\Delta x^{\mu}_{s}
in Eq. (
4.8
) is the expected transformation from its definition. We can then use its expression in Eqs. (
3.51
) and (
3.54
) to check the consistency of the gauge transformation.

4.1
Metric and observer velocity perturbations

The gauge transformations of the metric perturbations and the observer velocity will play a crucial role in the consistency check because all the second-order expressions in Section
3
finally depend on these quantities. We first apply the second-order gauge transformation equation (
4
) to the metric
g_{\mu\nu}
in Eq. (
2.1
) and the observer velocity
u^{\mu}
in Eq. (
2.7
). After straightforward computations, we obtain the second-order gauge transformations of the metric perturbations
[
93
]

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}-\left(T^{\prime}+\mathcal{H}T\right)-\mathcal{A}^{\prime}T-2\mathcal{A}\left(T^{\prime}+\mathcal{H}T\right)+{3\over 2}T^{\prime 2}+TT^{\prime\prime}+{1\over 2}\left(2\mathcal{H}^{2}+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\right)T^{2}

(4.9)

\displaystyle+3\mathcal{H}T^{\prime}T-\mathcal{A}_{,\alpha}\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}-\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}\mathcal{L}^{\prime\alpha}+T_{,\alpha}\mathcal{L}^{\prime\alpha}+\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}\left(T^{\prime}_{,\alpha}+\mathcal{H}T_{,\alpha}\right)-{1\over 2}\mathcal{L}^{\prime\alpha}\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\alpha}~,

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}-T_{,\alpha}+\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\alpha}-2\mathcal{A}T_{,\alpha}-\left(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{\alpha}+2\mathcal{H}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}\right)T-\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}T^{\prime}+2T^{\prime}T_{,\alpha}+T\left(T^{\prime}_{,\alpha}+2\mathcal{H}T_{,\alpha}\right)

(4.10)

\displaystyle-\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,\beta}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}-\mathcal{B}_{\beta}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{\;\;,\alpha}+2\mathcal{C}_{\alpha\beta}\mathcal{L}^{\prime\beta}-\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\alpha}T^{\prime}+T_{,\beta}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{\;\;,\alpha}+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}T_{,\alpha\beta}-T\left(\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}_{\alpha}+2\mathcal{H}\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle-2\mathcal{L}_{(\alpha,\beta)}\mathcal{L}^{\prime\beta}-\mathcal{L}^{\beta}\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\alpha,\beta}~,

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha\beta}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha\beta}-\mathcal{H}T\delta_{\alpha\beta}-\mathcal{L}_{(\alpha,\beta)}+\mathcal{B}_{(\alpha}T_{,\beta)}-\left(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}+2\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}_{\alpha\beta}\right)T+{1\over 2}\left(2\mathcal{H}^{2}+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\right)T^{2}\delta_{\alpha\beta}-{1\over 2}T_{,\alpha}T_{,\beta}

(4.11)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{H}TT^{\prime}\delta_{\alpha\beta}-\mathcal{C}_{\alpha\beta,\gamma}\mathcal{L}^{\gamma}-2\mathcal{C}_{\gamma(\alpha}\mathcal{L}^{\gamma}_{\;\;,\beta)}+\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{(\alpha}T_{,\beta)}+\mathcal{H}\mathcal{L}^{\gamma}T_{,\gamma}\delta_{\alpha\beta}+2\mathcal{H}\mathcal{L}_{(\alpha,\beta)}T+\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{(\alpha,\beta)}T

\displaystyle+\mathcal{L}^{\gamma}{}_{,(\alpha}\mathcal{L}_{\beta),\gamma}+\mathcal{L}^{\gamma}\mathcal{L}_{(\alpha,\beta)\gamma}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}^{\gamma}{}_{,\alpha}\mathcal{L}_{\gamma,\beta}\,,

where we used Eq. (
2.92
) to express the components of the gauge field
\xi^{\mu}
. Remember that we chose rectangular coordinates to simplify the calculations. In the same way, we obtain the second-order gauge transformation of the observer velocity

\displaystyle\widetilde{\delta u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta u+\mathcal{H}T+T^{\prime}+\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}-\mathcal{H}\mathcal{A}-T^{\prime\prime}\right)T+\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{H}^{2}-\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\right)T^{2}-\mathcal{A}T^{\prime}+\mathcal{U}^{\beta}T_{,\beta}

(4.12)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}\left(\mathcal{A}_{,\beta}-\mathcal{H}T_{,\beta}-T^{\prime}_{,\beta}\right)\,,

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}^{\alpha}+\mathcal{L}^{\prime\alpha}+\left(\mathcal{H}T-\mathcal{A}\right)\mathcal{L}^{\prime\alpha}+\left(\mathcal{H}\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}-\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime\alpha}-\mathcal{U}^{\prime\alpha}\right)T+\mathcal{U}^{\beta}\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}{}_{,\beta}

(4.13)

\displaystyle-\mathcal{L}^{\beta}\left(\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}{}_{,\beta}+\mathcal{L}^{\prime\alpha}{}_{,\beta}\right)\,.

We can readily check that Eq. (
4.12
) is consistent with the transformation obtained by directly substituting the gauge transformations of the metric perturbations derived above in the second-order expression for
\delta u
in Eq. (
3.6
).

Note that both sides of each equation presented in this subsection are evaluated at the same coordinate position
x^{\mu}
. For later convenience, we will assume these coordinates to be either on the background observer path
\bar{x}^{\mu}_{t}
or on the background light path
\bar{x}^{\mu}_{\bar{r}}
.

4.2
Time lapse and spatial shift at the observer position

We begin the consistency checks by computing the second-order transformations of the time lapse
\delta t_{o}
and spatial shift
\delta x^{\alpha}_{o}
at the observer position. On the one hand, we compute the transformations from the definitions of the lapse and shift in Eq. (
2.43
). On the other hand, we compute the transformations of the second-order expressions in Eqs. (
3.18
) and (
3.19
) in terms of metric and observer velocity perturbations. The former procedure will be denoted
second-order expectation
, while the latter
second-order direct computation
. We employ this terminology throughout the whole section. By checking that the two procedures give the same result, we ensure the sanity of our second-order expressions. We will only show the second-order gauge-transformation, from which the linear-order gauge transformation can be trivially read off.

\bullet

Second-order expectation
.—
Let us write in components the transformation of the time lapse and spatial shift in Eq. (
4.8
) in the observer position:

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\delta\eta}}_{o}=\delta\eta_{o}+T(\bar{x}_{\rm o})+\delta x^{\mu}_{o}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}T\right)\bigg\rvert_{\bar{x}_{\rm o}}\,,\qquad\quad{\widetilde{\delta x}}^{\alpha}_{o}=\delta x_{o}^{\alpha}+\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}(\bar{x}_{\rm o})+\delta x^{\mu}_{o}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}\right)\bigg\rvert_{\bar{x}_{\rm o}}\,.\qquad

(4.14)

To derive the expectation for the time lapse in proper time, let us use the relation between
\delta t_{o}
and
\delta\eta_{o}
in Eq. (
3.28
). Using the gauge transformation of
\delta\eta_{o}
above, we derive the expectation

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\delta t}}_{o}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\delta\eta}}_{o}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}_{\bar{\rm o}}\widetilde{\delta\eta}_{o}^{2}=\delta t_{o}+T_{\bar{\rm o}}+\delta x^{\mu}_{o}\left[{\partial\over\partial x^{\mu}}(aT)\right]\bigg\rvert_{\bar{\rm o}}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}_{\bar{\rm o}}T^{2}_{\bar{\rm o}}\,.

(4.15)

\bullet

Second-order direct computation
.—
We start the direct computations from the spatial shift at the observer position in Eq. (
3.19
). Directly substituting the gauge transformations of the metric perturbations and the observer velocity presented in Subsection
4.1
, and using the linear-order transformations of the coordinate perturbations in Eq. (
4.8
), we obtain

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\delta x}}^{\alpha}_{o}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{\delta x}{}_{o}^{\mu}\left(\partial_{\mu}\widetilde{\delta x}{}^{\alpha}\right)\bigg\rvert_{\bar{\rm o}}+\int_{0}^{\bar{t}_{\rm o}}dt\frac{1}{a}\left({\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}}^{\alpha}+\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\,\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\alpha}-\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\beta}\widetilde{\delta x}{}^{\alpha}{}_{,\beta}\right)

(4.16)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\delta x}^{\alpha}_{o}+\delta x^{\mu}_{o}(\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{L}^{\alpha})\big\rvert_{\bar{\rm o}}+\xi^{\mu}_{\bar{\rm o}}\left[\partial_{\mu}\left(\delta x^{\alpha}+\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}\right)\right]\big\rvert_{\bar{\rm o}}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\bar{t}_{\rm o}}dt\frac{1}{a}\bigg[\frac{\partial}{\partial\eta}\bigg(\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}-T\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}-T\mathcal{L}^{\prime\alpha}-\mathcal{L}^{\beta}\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}{}_{,\beta}\bigg)-\mathcal{L}^{\beta}\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}{}_{,\beta}-\mathcal{L}^{\prime\beta}\delta x^{\alpha}{}_{,\beta}\bigg]\bigg\rvert_{\bar{x}_{t}}\,,

where the boundary terms in the first line come exclusively from the gauge transformation of
\delta x_{o}^{\mu}(\partial_{\mu}\delta x^{\alpha})\big\rvert_{\bar{\rm o}}
.
From the linear-order condition in Eq. (
3.17
), valid at any point of the background observer path
\bar{x}^{\mu}_{t}
, we can rewrite the last two terms in the above integral as

\displaystyle-\mathcal{L}^{\beta}\mathcal{U}^{\alpha}{}_{,\beta}-\mathcal{L}^{\prime\beta}\delta x^{\alpha}{}_{,\beta}=-\frac{\partial}{\partial\eta}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}\delta x^{\alpha}{}_{,\beta}\right)\,.

(4.17)

With this result and the relation
dt=a\,d\eta
, we notice that the integrand above is a partial derivative with respect to cosmic time
t
, and the integration yields boundary terms evaluated at the background observer position. Most of the terms canceled in the resulting expression to yield

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\delta x}}^{\alpha}_{o}={\delta x}^{\alpha}_{o}+\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}_{\bar{\rm o}}+\delta x_{o}^{\mu}\left({\partial\over\partial x^{\mu}}\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}\right)\bigg\rvert_{\bar{\rm o}}\,,

(4.18)

which indeed coincides with the second-order expectation.

We can proceed in the same way for the time lapse at the observer position. We have derived two expressions for the second-order lapse, the general expression in Eq. (
3.13
) and the simplified expression in Eq. (
3.26
) under the assumption that the observer motion is geodesic. We will compute the direct transformation of the general expression:

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\delta t}}_{o}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{\delta x}{}^{\mu}_{o}(\partial_{\mu}\widetilde{\delta t})\big\rvert_{\bar{\rm o}}+\int_{0}^{\bar{t}_{\rm o}}dt\left(-{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}+\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\alpha}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{\alpha}-\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\alpha}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\alpha}-\frac{1}{a}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\alpha}\widetilde{\delta t}_{,\alpha}\right)~

(4.19)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\delta t}_{o}+\delta x^{\mu}_{o}[\partial_{\mu}(aT)]\big\rvert_{\bar{\rm o}}+\xi^{\mu}_{\bar{\rm o}}[\partial_{\mu}\big(\delta t+aT\big)]\big\rvert_{\bar{\rm o}}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\bar{t}_{\rm o}}dt\,\bigg[\frac{1}{a}\frac{\partial}{\partial\eta}\bigg(aT+a\mathcal{A}T-a\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}T_{,\alpha}-aTT^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}a\mathcal{H}T^{2}\bigg)+\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}\mathcal{A}_{,\alpha}-\frac{1}{a}\mathcal{L}^{\prime\alpha}\delta t_{,\alpha}\bigg]\,.\quad\qquad

Again, we can use the linear-order condition in Eq. (
3.17
), valid on the observer background path, to rearrange the last two terms in the integral

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}\mathcal{A}_{,\alpha}-\frac{1}{a}\mathcal{L}^{\prime\alpha}\delta t_{,\alpha}=-\frac{1}{a}\frac{\partial}{\partial\eta}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}\delta t_{,\alpha}\right)\,,

(4.20)

such that the integration yields boundary terms at the observer position. Simplifying the resulting expression, we obtain

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\delta t}}_{o}=\delta t_{o}+T_{\bar{\rm o}}+\delta x^{\mu}_{o}\left[{\partial\over\partial x^{\mu}}(aT)\right]\bigg\rvert_{\bar{\rm o}}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}_{\bar{\rm o}}T^{2}_{\bar{\rm o}}\,,

(4.21)

in agreement with the expected result (see
[
92
]
).

4.3
Distortion in the conformal wave vector

Here we want to compute the second-order gauge transformation of the components of the conformal light wave vector
\hat{k}^{\mu}
defined in Eq. (
2.30
). In the real universe
\mathcal{M}
, we have argued that the conformal light wave vector is
not
an actual vector, and its components
\delta\nu
and
{\delta n}^{\alpha}
depend on the position on the light path
x^{\mu}_{\lambda}
and the reference position
x^{\mu}_{o}
. As far as gauge transformations are concerned, we are considering an active change of coordinates only for the position on the light path, while the reference is fixed at the physical point
O
in both frames.
6
6

6

Note that if we also expanded
\widetilde{x}^{\mu}_{o}
around
x^{\mu}_{o}
, this position would not correspond to the point
O
in the frame denoted by a tilde.
For this reason, in this section, we will consider the perturbations as a function of the light path only:
\delta\nu=\delta\nu(x_{\lambda})\,,\,{\delta n}^{\alpha}={\delta n}^{\alpha}(x_{\lambda})
.

\bullet

Second-order expectation
.—
To compute the gauge transformation of
\hat{k}^{\mu}
, we first compute the gauge transformation of
k^{\mu}
parameterized as in Eq. (
2.31
), since the light wave vector
k^{\mu}
is a four-vector in
\mathcal{M}
while
\hat{k}^{\mu}
is not. Mind the following subtle point: the left-hand side of Eq. (
2.31
) is evaluated at a point
x^{\mu}_{\Lambda}
in the real universe, while the right-hand side depends on
x^{\mu}_{\lambda}
and
x^{\mu}_{o}
. Consequently, a change of coordinates in a point of the real universe implies a change of coordinates in two points of the conformal universe.

To compute the second-order expectation of
\widetilde{\delta}\nu
and
\widetilde{\delta n}{}^{\alpha}
we employ the transformation law for the components of a tensor evaluated at a point
P
, given in Eq. (
2.88
). This equation provides the expression for a tensor of arbitrary rank; in the case of a four-vector, it reads

\displaystyle\widetilde{k}^{\mu}(\widetilde{x}_{p})=\left(\frac{\partial\widetilde{x}^{\mu}}{\partial x^{\nu}}\right)_{p}k^{\nu}(x_{p})\,.

(4.22)

In principle, we could also apply Eq. (
4
) directly to derive the sought gauge transformations, but with this procedure we will derive intermediate relations that will be crucial for the consistency check. Substituting in the above equation the parameterization of
k^{\mu}
in Eq. (
2.31
) we derive

\displaystyle 1+\widetilde{\delta}\nu(\widetilde{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{a^{2}(\widetilde{\eta})}{a^{2}(\eta)}\frac{a(\eta_{o})}{a(\widetilde{\eta}_{o})}\frac{\partial\widetilde{\eta}}{\partial x^{\nu}}\bigg(1+\delta\nu(x)\,,\,-n^{\alpha}-{\delta n}^{\alpha}(x)\bigg)^{\nu}

(4.23)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{a^{2}(\widetilde{\eta})}{a^{2}(\eta)}\frac{a(\eta_{o})}{a(\widetilde{\eta}_{o})}\Big[\left(1+T^{\prime}\right)\left(1+\delta\nu\right)-T_{,\alpha}\left(n^{\alpha}+{\delta n}^{\alpha}\right)\Big](x)\,,

for the zero-th component
\mu=\eta
, and

\displaystyle-n^{\alpha}-\widetilde{\delta n}{}^{\alpha}(\widetilde{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{a^{2}(\widetilde{\eta})}{a^{2}(\eta)}\frac{a(\eta_{o})}{a(\widetilde{\eta}_{o})}\frac{\partial\widetilde{x}^{\alpha}}{\partial x^{\nu}}\bigg(1+\delta\nu(x)\,,\,-n^{\alpha}-{\delta n}^{\alpha}(x)\bigg)^{\nu}

(4.24)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{a^{2}(\widetilde{\eta})}{a^{2}(\eta)}\frac{a(\eta_{o})}{a(\widetilde{\eta}_{o})}\Big[\mathcal{L}^{\prime\alpha}\left(1+\delta\nu\right)-\left(\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}+\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}{}_{,\beta}\right)\left(n^{\beta}+{\delta n}^{\beta}\right)\Big](x)\,,

for the spatial components
\mu=\alpha
. In both equations, we suppressed the subscript
p
, and we used
\widetilde{\omega}_{o}=\omega_{o}
, as the observed frequency does not depend on our coordinate description. Note that these equations are valid at any perturbative order at this stage and
n^{\alpha}
is the observed angle in the rest frame of the observer, not a function of position
x^{\mu}
. By expanding
\widetilde{x}^{\mu}
in Eq. (
2.91
) around
x^{\mu}
in the right-hand side, we derive at the second order:

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\delta}\nu}(\widetilde{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta\nu-\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}T+\left(2\mathcal{H}T-\mathcal{H}_{o}T_{o}\right)\left(1+\delta\nu-\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}T\right)+\left(2\mathcal{H}^{2}+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\right)T^{2}

(4.25)

\displaystyle+T^{\prime}\delta\nu-T_{,\alpha}{\delta n}^{\alpha}-2\mathcal{H}\mathcal{H}_{o}TT_{o}+\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{H}_{o}^{2}-\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{o}\right)T_{o}^{2}\,,

\displaystyle{\widetilde{{\delta n}}}{}^{\alpha}(\widetilde{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\delta n}^{\alpha}+\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}+\left(2\mathcal{H}T-\mathcal{H}_{o}T_{o}\right)\left(n^{\alpha}+{\delta n}^{\alpha}+\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}\right)-\mathcal{L}^{\prime\alpha}\delta\nu+\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}{}_{,\beta}{\delta n}^{\beta}

(4.26)

\displaystyle+\left(2\mathcal{H}^{2}+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\right)T^{2}n^{\alpha}+\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{H}_{o}^{2}-\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{o}\right)T_{o}^{2}n^{\alpha}-2\mathcal{H}\mathcal{H}_{o}TT_{o}n^{\alpha}\,,

which will be used to check the validity of our expressions. Note that the left-hand side is evaluated at
\widetilde{x}^{\mu}
, while the right-hand side at
x^{\mu}
. To obtain the gauge-transformation relation at the same value, we first expand the left-hand side around
x^{\mu}
, move the quadratic terms to the right-hand side, and finally use the linear-order relation:

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\delta}\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta\nu-\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}T+T\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}T^{\prime}+\left(2\mathcal{H}T-\mathcal{H}_{o}T_{o}\right)\left(1+\delta\nu-\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}T\right)

(4.27)

\displaystyle-\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}\left(\delta\nu_{,\alpha}+2\mathcal{H}T_{,\alpha}-\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}T_{,\alpha}\right)+\left(2\mathcal{H}^{2}-\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\right)T^{2}-2\mathcal{H}TT^{\prime}

\displaystyle+T^{\prime}\delta\nu-T_{,\alpha}{\delta n}^{\alpha}-T\delta\nu^{\prime}-2\mathcal{H}\mathcal{H}_{o}TT_{o}+\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{H}_{o}^{2}-\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{o}\right)T_{o}^{2}\,,

\displaystyle{\widetilde{{\delta n}}}{}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\delta n}^{\alpha}+\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}-T\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}\mathcal{L}^{\prime\alpha}+\left(2\mathcal{H}T-\mathcal{H}_{o}T_{o}\right)\left(n^{\alpha}+{\delta n}^{\alpha}+\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}\right)

(4.28)

\displaystyle-\mathcal{L}^{\beta}\left({\delta n}^{\alpha}{}_{,\beta}+2\mathcal{H}T_{,\beta}n^{\alpha}+\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}{}_{,\beta}\right)-2\mathcal{H}TT^{\prime}n^{\alpha}+\left(2\mathcal{H}^{2}-\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\right)T^{2}n^{\alpha}

\displaystyle-\mathcal{L}^{\prime\alpha}\delta\nu+\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}{}_{,\beta}{\delta n}^{\beta}-T{\delta n}^{\prime\alpha}+\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{H}_{o}^{2}-\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{o}\right)T_{o}^{2}n^{\alpha}-2\mathcal{H}\mathcal{H}_{o}TT_{o}n^{\alpha}\,.

As a concluding remark we stress again that the coordinates of the reference position have not been expanded as they refer to the physical point
O
. In practice, this means that in the left-hand side the equations read
\widetilde{\delta}\nu(\widetilde{x}=x;\widetilde{x}_{o})\,,\,\ \widetilde{\delta n}^{\alpha}(\widetilde{x}=x;\widetilde{x}_{o})
, while the right-hand sides contains only fields evaluated at
x^{\mu}
and
x^{\mu}_{o}
.

\bullet

Second-order direct computation
.—
Our second-order expressions for
\delta\nu
and
{\delta n}^{\alpha}
are given in Eq. (
3.35
) and
Eq. (
3.36
) respectively. We first check the consistency of the expression for
\delta\nu
by computing the gauge transformation

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\delta}\nu}(\widetilde{x}_{s})-{\widetilde{\delta}\nu}(\widetilde{x}_{o})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\widetilde{\Delta}x{}^{\mu}_{\bar{r}}(\partial_{\mu}\widetilde{\delta}\nu)\big\rvert_{\bar{x}_{\bar{r}}}\right]^{z}_{\bar{\rm o}}+\int_{0}^{\bar{r}_{z}}d\bar{r}\left({\widetilde{\delta}\hat{\Gamma}}{}^{\eta}+\widetilde{\delta}\nu\widetilde{\delta}\nu^{\prime}-\widetilde{\delta n}{}^{\alpha}\widetilde{\delta}\nu_{,\alpha}\right)\bigg\rvert_{\bar{x}_{\bar{r}}}

(4.29)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg\{\Delta x^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\left(\delta\nu+2\mathcal{H}T-\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}T\right)+T\delta\nu^{\prime}+\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}\delta\nu_{,\alpha}-T\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}T^{\prime}

\displaystyle-\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}T_{,\alpha}+2\mathcal{H}^{\prime}T^{2}+2\mathcal{H}TT^{\prime}+2\mathcal{H}\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}T_{,\alpha}\bigg\}\bigg\rvert^{z}_{\bar{\rm o}}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\bar{r}_{z}}d\bar{r}\left({\widetilde{\delta}\hat{\Gamma}}{}^{\eta}+\widetilde{\delta}\nu\widetilde{\delta}\nu^{\prime}-\widetilde{\delta n}{}^{\alpha}\widetilde{\delta}\nu_{,\alpha}\right)\bigg\rvert_{\bar{x}_{\bar{r}}}\,,

where only the boundary terms have been expanded, for which we used the gauge transformation of
\Delta x^{\mu}
in Eq. (
4.8
) and the linear-order expression of
\delta\nu
in Eq. (
3.4
). We present the gauge transformation of the integral separately to avoid lengthy expressions:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\bar{r}_{z}}d\bar{r}\,\left({\widetilde{\delta}\hat{\Gamma}}{}^{\eta}+\widetilde{\delta}\nu\widetilde{\delta}\nu^{\prime}-\widetilde{\delta n}{}^{\alpha}\widetilde{\delta}\nu_{,\alpha}\right)\bigg\rvert_{\bar{x}_{\bar{r}}}=

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\int_{0}^{\bar{r}_{z}}d\bar{r}\,\left(\delta\hat{\Gamma}^{\eta}+\delta\nu\delta\nu^{\prime}-{\delta n}^{\alpha}\delta\nu_{,\alpha}\right)\bigg\rvert_{\bar{x}_{\bar{r}}}+\bigg\{-\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}T+T\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}T^{\prime}+2\mathcal{H}T\left(1+\delta\nu-\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}T\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad-2\mathcal{H}TT^{\prime}-\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}\left(\delta\nu_{,\alpha}+2\mathcal{H}T_{,\alpha}-\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}T_{,\alpha}\right)-\mathcal{H}_{o}T_{o}\left(\delta\nu-\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}T\right)+\left(2\mathcal{H}^{2}-\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\right)T^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad-T\delta\nu^{\prime}+T^{\prime}\delta\nu-T_{,\alpha}{\delta n}^{\alpha}-2\mathcal{H}\mathcal{H}_{o}TT_{o}\bigg\}\bigg\rvert^{z}_{\bar{\rm o}}\,,

(4.30)

where we used
\delta\hat{\Gamma}^{\eta}
in Eq. (
3.1
),
\delta\nu
and
{\delta n}^{\alpha}
in Eq. (
3.4
), and we stress that all the fields inside the curly brackets are evaluated along the background light path
\bar{x}^{\mu}_{\bar{r}}
. Combining the two previous equations (
4.29
) and (
4.3
), we obtain after straightforward simplifications:

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\delta}\nu}(\widetilde{x}_{s})-{\widetilde{\delta}\nu}(\widetilde{x}_{o})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\delta\nu}(x_{s})-{\delta\nu}(x_{o})+\bigg\{-\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}T+2\mathcal{H}T+\Delta x^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\left(2\mathcal{H}T-\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}T\right)

(4.31)

\displaystyle+T^{\prime}\delta\nu-T_{,\alpha}{\delta n}^{\alpha}+\left(2\mathcal{H}T-\mathcal{H}_{o}T_{o}\right)\left(\delta\nu-\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}T\right)+\left(2\mathcal{H}^{2}+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\right)T^{2}

\displaystyle-2\mathcal{H}\mathcal{H}_{o}TT_{o}\bigg\}\bigg\rvert^{z}_{\bar{\rm o}}\,.

The second-order expectation for this quantity is given by evaluating Eq. (
4.25
) at the source position
P=S
and the observer position
P=O
. The difference of these two contributions yields

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\delta}\nu}(\widetilde{x_{s}})-{\widetilde{\delta}\nu}(\widetilde{x_{o}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\delta\nu}(x_{s})-{\delta\nu}(x_{o})+\bigg\{-\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}T+2\mathcal{H}T+T^{\prime}\delta\nu-T_{,\alpha}{\delta n}^{\alpha}

(4.32)

\displaystyle+\left(2\mathcal{H}T-\mathcal{H}_{o}T_{o}\right)\left(\delta\nu-\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}T\right)+\left(2\mathcal{H}^{2}+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\right)T^{2}-2\mathcal{H}\mathcal{H}_{o}TT_{o}\bigg\}\bigg\rvert^{s}_{o}\,.\qquad

There seems to be a discrepancy between the two equations but note the different evaluation positions. The fields in the curly brackets in Eq. (
4.31
) are evaluated at coordinates
\bar{x}^{\mu}_{z}
and
\bar{x}^{\mu}_{o}
along the background paths
\bar{x}^{\mu}_{\bar{r}}
. On the other hand, the fields in the curly brackets of the expectation in Eq. (
4.32
) are evaluated at coordinates
x^{\mu}_{s}
and
x^{\mu}_{o}
along the real path
x^{\mu}_{\lambda}
.
When we discussed subtleties at second order in Subsection
3.2
, we argued that evaluating a field either on the real path or on the background path makes a difference at second order, and the discrepancy is given in Eq. (
3.10
). Hence, we need the following second-order relations to compare the two equations properly:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}T\bigg\rvert_{x}=\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}T\bigg\rvert_{\bar{x}}+\Delta x^{\mu}\left[\partial_{\mu}\left(\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}T\right)\right]\bigg\rvert_{\bar{x}}\,,\qquad(\mathcal{H}T)(x)=\mathcal{H}(\bar{\eta})T(\bar{x})+\Delta x^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\left(\mathcal{H}T\right)\bigg\rvert_{\bar{x}}\,,\quad

(4.33)

where we do not show any subscript as these equations are valid both for source and observer positions. Moreover, with
\Delta x^{\mu}
we refer both to
\Delta x^{\mu}_{s}
and
\delta x^{\mu}_{o}
.
Substituting these relations in Eq. (
4.32
), we readily check that the expectation and the direct computation agree and indeed the apparent discrepancy was just a consequence of different evaluation positions.

We can repeat the same procedure for the spatial perturbation
{\delta n}^{\alpha}
given in Eq. (
3.36
). The transformation of the boundary terms is given by

\displaystyle\left[\widetilde{\Delta}x{}^{\mu}_{\bar{r}}(\partial_{\mu}\widetilde{\delta n}{}^{\alpha})\big\rvert_{\bar{x}_{\bar{r}}}\right]^{z}_{\bar{\rm o}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg\{\Delta x^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\left({\delta n}^{\alpha}+2\mathcal{H}Tn^{\alpha}+\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}\right)+T{\delta n}^{\prime\alpha}+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}{\delta n}^{\alpha}{}_{,\beta}+T\frac{d}{d\bar{r}}\mathcal{L}^{\prime\alpha}

(4.34)
